Requirement identifiers (Rn.m, PR-nn, ASM-nn, ORQ-nn, RN-nn) point into requirements.md. TBR-nn, GAP-nn, and KS-nn point into the Trust_Base_Register `docs/trust_base_register.toml`, the Gap_Register `docs/gap_register.toml`, and `docs/kani_stubs.toml`. Both registers cite the P-nn rows of section 3, and `cargo xtask check` fails when a cited row, requirement, ASM, ORQ, or gap does not exist (R48, R54). "Proof" means Verus (all reachable states) or Kani (all inputs within stated bounds); "test" means testing evidence (R43.4); "argument" means a pen-and-paper argument recorded in section 5, which is review evidence and earns no DO-333 credit until mechanized.

## 2. What the type system gives (R43.3)

Relied on, given sound `unsafe` code and a correct compiler (ASM-14, TBR-21):

- data-race freedom in safe code through `Send` and `Sync`;
- closure-scoped LIFO locking with no escaping references (R8.6), no re-entry (R8.10), and Resource access only by declared Tasks (R8.7), through the `rsk` API shape;
- synchronous Task bodies (PR-09) and, from Phase 4, Endpoint protocol states within a Job (R38.4).

Not given, and allocated in section 3: deadlock freedom, bounded blocking, timing, schedulability, panic freedom, stack bounds, and freedom from leaks.

**A plain-English companion (2026-11-16): `docs/requirements_evidence.md`.** One row per requirement, first for those the paper's claims rest on (R10, R14, R16, R17, R18, R29 with Property 18, R41): what the requirement asks, what implements it, what each cited contract or theorem states, the kinds of evidence, whether the statements are as strong as the requirement, and what remains. The sentences are written by hand in `docs/requirements_evidence.toml` from the statements' text; the identifiers, files, lines, statement names and Evidence_Items are generated by `cargo xtask reqdoc` from the data of the traceability matrix and from the cited sources. Each cited statement's text is hashed, comments and layout aside, and the row records the hash it was written against: a statement that changes afterwards marks its row in the document, fails `cargo xtask reqdoc --check`, and is a violation of the verification job naming the row (the step that writes the matrix), until the row is read again and the new hash entered. Two things the first reading found, both recorded in the rows as "weaker": the contract of `dma_request_core` does not state the refusal rule of R17.2 (it states the Kernel's invariant and three unchanged processor settings; the rule is in the function's body and on the rig), and no row's statements bound a detection or response time (those are measured). The first was closed the same day: `dma_request_core` now ensures that a request is accepted exactly when `dma_accepts` holds (the channel is the executing Job's own Partition's and the buffer is DMA-permitted memory of it), that an accepted request adds exactly that channel's registers and that buffer to the model's record of programmed transfers (`HwModel::dma`, which `Action::DmaProgram` alone extends), and that a refused one leaves the record as it was; the channel table has a specification (`spec_dma_of`, `spec_dma_count_of`) as the Endpoint table has. Checked by Verus on `rsk-kernel` alone (274 verified, 0 errors; `dma_stop_partition` given a solver instance of its own, as `dma_request_core` has, after its loop passed the default resource limit in the shared context); three mutants registered, each run alone and killed (the buffer check dropped, the Partition check dropped, a refusal that programs the registers). The same statement for `dma_write_core` (it writes no pointer, count or list-mode register) is still only in its body.

### Testing-only evidence (R43.4)

| Row | Why no proof tool applies |
|---|---|
| P-11 | Preservation is done by hardware stacking (HW-10) and assembly stubs. Verus has no assembly and Kani does not model it (RN-16). |
| P-07 (jitter), P-17 (Δ_detect, Δ_enforce), P-14 (hardware boot checks) | Physical timing and silicon behaviour, which a source-level proof cannot observe. |
| P-20 | No static WCET tool before P5 (ORQ-12); measurement is not a bound (ASM-11). |
| P-24 | Loom and Shuttle are explicitly not sound (RN-37): section 6.4 records their limitations and labels every result testing evidence (R47.3). |
| P-25 | A model cannot validate itself against silicon. |

## 4. What holds by inspection (the Kernel_Arch boundary)

Since 2026-10-22 (task 5.7) every Kernel_Arch entry point is a verified core in `arch/cm4/entry.rs`, generic over `KernelOps` (the bound Kernel, `arch/bound.rs`), `KernelArch` (the hardware, driven through `Action`s whose specification is a step of the ghost `HwModel`, the Kernel's record of what it wrote), and `Tables` (the generated system crate's inputs). Outside `verus!` remain: the shells in `arch/cm4/mod.rs` (fetch the Kernel, the hardware, the tables, and the Kernel_Arch state; call the core; enter `System::safe_state`, which never returns), the `System` trait and its `Tables` adapter, the Kernel_Unsafe_Module (register access, assembly stubs), and rsk-entry. The register-facing surface of the planning code is the `Cm4Hw` implementation of `KernelArch`: seven trusted functions (`apply`, `now`, `cycles`, `boot_checks`, `boot_info`, and since task 21.2 `copy_in` and `copy_out`; `#[verifier::external_body]`, listed in `docs/proof_assumptions.toml`), of which `apply` calls exactly one register-access function per action.

| # | Fact | Assumed by | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| A1 | BASEPRI = `basepri_for(top, ceiling)` after every transition | P-04, P-17 | **Proven at the model** (`basepri_matches`): `apply_core`, the dispatch path of `irq_entry_core`, `svc_core` (lock, unlock), `timer_core`, `epilogue_core`, and `fault_core` end with the model's BASEPRI equal to `hw_model::basepri_for(job_level, ceiling)`, or 0 in the safe state. Trusted: `Cm4Hw::apply(SetBasepri(v))` writes `v` to BASEPRI (`hw::write_basepri`, UJ-010) |
| A2 | PRIMASK is set only by Kernel stubs and `start` and is clear on every return to Thread mode | P-04, P-17 | `start_core` proven to clear PRIMASK (model) unless the safe state is entered; the assembly stubs by inspection (`stubs.rs`, UJ-011, UJ-012) |
| A3 | Every dispatch vector has NVIC priority `encode_level(priority)`; every Kernel exception has priority 0 | P-04, P-17 | By inspection and test, not proven (corrected 2026-11-13, audit finding 12: `HwModel.priority` appears in no contract, so no Verus postcondition states which priority a vector received): `boot_core` programs each bound vector at `encode_level(priority)` (dispatch) or `encode_level(0)` (intake, Kernel) and applies `KernelExceptionPriorities` (model flag, in `booted`); the value `encode_level` is a proven computation; the IPR and SHPR writes trusted (`Cm4Hw::apply`); HIL: `hil-dt-hw-02`, `-03` |
| A4 | Only the vector `Kernel::select` returns is pended; a stale pending bit is rejected at entry (argument 5.8) | P-04 | The selection and the eligibility test are proven computations (`KernelOps::select`, `eligible`); that `apply_core` pends exactly `tables.dispatch_vector(t)` for that `t` and that `irq_entry_core` refuses a Task `eligible` rejects is by inspection and test (corrected 2026-11-13, audit finding 12: `HwModel.pending` appears in no contract). Since 5.8a a dispatch vector taken at another stub's entry instruction is disabled, and re-enabled pending by the next entry from Thread mode (audit finding 15): the vector pended is still the selected one. That `dispatch_vector(t)` is Task t's vector: the Generator (A12) |
| A5 | FAULTMASK is never set; Jobs run with CONTROL = 0b001 | P-17 | Proven: a dispatch returns `CONTROL_JOB` (0b001) and `EXC_RETURN_THREAD_PSP`. FAULTMASK (restated 2026-11-13, audit finding 15): no `Action` touches it, but since 5.8a every stub sets it (`cpsid f`) for its return window, and the exception return clears it; A5 therefore reads "FAULTMASK is set only inside a Kernel stub's return window". That an exception return clears FAULTMASK (Armv7-M ARM B1.4.3) has no Hardware_Model rule and no differential test (R49.6): an inspection fact, recorded in the Hardware_Model's not-formalized list |
| A6 | The views are `Layout::view` of a layout that passed `validate`, loaded at dispatch and resume, with the MPU enabled from boot | P-16 | Proven: `Bound::inv` (every view `is_view_of` the validated layout), and `boot_core` enables the MPU (model flag, in `booted`). By inspection and test (corrected 2026-11-13, audit finding 12: the loaded view's value appears in no postcondition; a core that omitted the restore would still verify): `irq_entry_core` loads the dispatched Job's Partition view and `epilogue_core` the resumed Job's; HIL: the isolation matrix, whose every attempt runs after dispatches and resumes. Trusted: `Cm4Hw::apply(LoadView)` is `hw::mpu_load` |
| A7 | RBAR and RASR words encode the view | P-16 | Kani (field placement) and review: `hw::rasr_encode`, `rbar_encode`, `mpu_load` |
| A8 | The time-base TIMER, dispatch vectors, and system handlers are never disabled after boot | P-17 | By inspection of the cores (now a few lines): `Disable` is applied to intake vectors from `tables.intake_vector`, to the entered vector of an intake or unbound exception, and in `boot_core` before programming. Since 5.8a `irq_entry_core` also disables a dispatch vector taken at another dispatch stub's entry instruction and re-enables it, pending, at the next entry from Thread mode (audit finding 15, 2026-11-13): A8 holds for the time base and the system handlers always, and for a dispatch vector outside that window, in which the deferred vector could not have been taken usefully. That intake vectors are distinct from dispatch and timer vectors: the Generator |
| A9 | The runtime guards in `Bound` mirror the `requires` clauses of the logic entry points | every Kernel proof | **Proven**: Verus checks every call in `arch/bound.rs` and every call of the cores into `KernelOps` |
| A10 | The Kernel cell is never entered reentrantly (DD-02) | every Kernel proof | By inspection: UJ-003 to UJ-006 (review) |
| A11 | `placeholder_kernel` is replaced by `init` before the first transition | `inv` at the first call | **Proven**: `boot_core` calls `validate` then `init` before any other `KernelOps` method and establishes `inv`; the mutation `boot-skips-init` shows a dropped `init` failing. That rsk-entry calls `boot` before `start` and before enabling exceptions: by inspection (rsk-entry) |

A12, the ascending dispatch-vector order (DD-03, `dispatch_vectors_ok`), is checked by the rsk-gen test `dispatch_irqs_ascend_for_dd03` for every built-in Target, not by inspection.

A13 (Property 9's hardware half, 2026-11-05): an intake entry for source `t` is taken only while its vector is enabled (the NVIC, HW-02), the entry disables the vector first (`irq_entry_core`), and after every entry `apply_core` enables the vector of each intake source exactly when the source is unmasked and its Partition not stopped (`source_masked`, `partition_stopped`). By inspection of `apply_core`'s intake loop; the loop is not proven because `Tables` states no distinctness of intake vectors, which the Generator guarantees (one line per source).

Earlier in task 5.7 (2026-10-22, `arch/cm4/plan.rs`): the new Job's frame address is proven 8-byte aligned (HW-06), at or above the Partition stack's bottom and wholly below the floor (R16.5), and the highest such address (`plan_frame`; the overflow case raises the Kernel fault instead of writing below the region); the TIMER compare value is proven equal to `hw_model::compare_for` (HW-12, `compare_target`); the unwind's level lookup is proven to return the greatest matching level (`find_level`; replaced on 2026-11-13 by `top_active`, the greatest active level, which `entry::lemma_top_active` proves to be the last Job dispatched and not yet unwound, 5.16).

Crate layout for proof stability (2026-10-22): the proof core (`logic`, `hw_model`) is the crate `rsk-logic`, and Kernel_Arch (`arch::bound`, `arch::cm4`, the Kernel_Unsafe_Module) is `rsk-kernel`, which re-exports it. Verus verifies each crate's items in its own SMT context and imports the other's specifications only, so a change on one side cannot destabilize the other's proofs; the `rlimit` raise that `end_top` needed in the single crate was removed after the split and the proof passes at the default limit. The explicit array-fill axiom applications in `Slots::new` and `Layout::view` are kept as cheap robustness.

What the model does not cover, and the evidence that does: the hardware's own response to the written state (the NVIC taking a vector, the MPU decision) is the Hardware_Model's domain (`hw_model` lemmas over the values the cores are proven to write); the stub-level register save and restore (Property 2, review UJ-020 to UJ-023 and the HIL register-pattern test); the five trusted functions (Kani harnesses on the encoders and address table, host tests of the boot checks, HIL differential tests, section 11). Trust_Base_Register entry TBR-24 lists the remaining facts; Gap_Register entry GAP-10 is mitigated for the planning code and stays open for the Kani flight-path restructuring.

## 5. Arguments recorded here

These close gaps that the Verus specifications leave between single transitions. Each is review evidence (TBR-06 style), cites the proven facts it uses, and names what mechanizing it would take. Mechanizing 5.1 to 5.5 is tasks 4.12 to 4.14: the ghost history they need exists since 2026-10-22 (task 4.11, `logic::history`): `Kernel::history` records every dispatch, Job end, lock, unlock, Release due, and kill with its observed instant; `Kernel::inv` carries the trace invariant `history_wf` and every entry point ensures that it only appended to the history. Arguments 5.1 to 5.5 are mechanized (tasks 4.12 to 4.14, below); each entry names what stays outside its theorems.

### 5.1 Bounded blocking: one Critical_Section per Release (R8.4)

Let J be a Job of Priority p released at r, pending but not eligible only because p ≤ System_Ceiling.

1. While J is pending, no Job of Priority below p starts. `Sched::select` takes the highest-Priority pending Release, and if that one is not eligible no pending Release is (proven); a lower-Priority Job is not eligible while p ≤ ceiling.
2. By `lemma_blocked_by_one_holder`, some lock entry with Ceiling ≥ p is held by a Job L of Priority below p, and by step 1 L started before r.
3. At most one such L exists. If L₁ below L₂ on the stack both held Resources with Ceiling ≥ p, then L₂ started above the ceiling of that instant (invariant 1), which is at least the Ceiling of L₁'s Resource (invariant 4), so Priority(L₂) > p, a contradiction.
4. When L's outermost unlock drops the ceiling below p, `apply` pends J's vector at once (A4, P-04) and J preempts L before L executes again, so L cannot lock a second Resource with Ceiling ≥ p in J's way.

So J waits for at most one outermost Critical_Section (with its nested sections) of one lower-Priority Job, plus Kernel overhead. The PBT against the SRP simulator (task 15.4, 2026-10-23: `crates/rsk-analyzer/tests/properties.rs`, Property 18) observes no response time above the Analyzer's bound on any of more than 100 passing random sets, with and without injected Overruns; see 5.10.

**Mechanized 2026-10-23 (task 4.12), in four lemmas.** Every step of the ghost history now records the executing Task after it (`top`); `history_wf` relates consecutive tops by the step's kind (`top_rel`: a dispatch makes its Task the top and, as `Kernel::dispatch` requires, that Task was eligible after the previous step, above the recorded ceiling and the recorded top; a lock or unlock is by the top and keeps it); `Kernel::inv` ties the last recorded top to the scheduler (`top_match`). The premise of the run-level lemmas is that J waits: its Release is step `r`, and `d` is any later index (corrected 2026-11-13, the audit's contested finding E: `d` is a free index of the lemmas, not constrained to be J's dispatch step, so the lemmas instantiate at the first index after which J is eligible; the window from that index to J's dispatch rests on the hardware argument of A4 and GAP-14), and after no step before `d - 1` is J eligible on the recorded ceiling and top (`eligible_after`); that premise is the Kernel_Arch promptness of A4 (an eligible Job is pended at once, `apply_core`) together with J's Release staying pending, and is the caller's. Step 1: `history::lemma_no_lower_dispatch_while_waiting`, no Job of Priority below p starts in the window. Step 3: `Sched::lemma_one_low_blocker`, at most one Job of Priority below p holds an entry with Ceiling at least p, in every state. Step 4: `history::lemma_no_new_blocking_section`, a lock in the window by a Job of Priority below p finds the System_Ceiling already at least p (so it opens no new blocking section), and `Sched::lemma_low_locker_nested`, in that state the locker itself holds the entry that blocks J (so the lock is nested in its own open section). Step 2 is `lemma_blocked_by_one_holder` as before. Together: the only blocking section is the one open at J's Release, held by one lower-Priority Job, and it is never followed by another before J starts. What remains outside the theorems is the premise above and the Kernel overhead term. The mutations `lock-by-other-than-top` and `dispatch-below-ceiling` fail steps 4 and 1.

### 5.2 Lazy release of killed Jobs delays nothing (R8.9, R14.5)

`Sched::kill` marks a preempted Job K but leaves its lock entries until Kernel_Arch unwinds to it, when `finish_top` releases them. Every Job above K started above the ceiling of its start instant (invariant 1), which covers the Ceilings of K's Resources (invariant 4). A Job X blocked only by K's retained Resources has Priority at most their Ceiling, so below the executing Job's Priority, and is not eligible while any Job above K is in progress. When the last Job above K ends, the unwind reaches K and releases its entries in the same Kernel entry (`epilogue`). The retained entries therefore never delay a Job that the Jobs above K would not delay anyway. Assumes A1 and the unwind order of the stubs (UJ-023, review).

**Mechanized 2026-10-22 (task 4.13):** `Sched::lemma_lazy_release_delays_nothing`, the logic content of the argument as a theorem over any state satisfying `Sched::inv`: with Job K at stack position `k` killed and some Job in progress above it, a pending Task X that the System_Ceiling blocks although no entry of a holder other than K blocks it has Priority below every Job above K, the executing one included, so X is not eligible whether or not K's entries are released. The proof uses the attained maximum of the mirror (`lemma_max_ceiling_attained`), invariant 4 (K's entries have Ceilings at most the start ceiling of each Job above K), and invariant 1. The mutation `lazy-release-no-job-above` (dropping the Job above K) fails it. What stays outside the theorem: that the unwind to K releases the entries in the same Kernel entry, which is `epilogue_core` (Verification_Plan section 4) and the stub order UJ-023.

### 5.3 Ceiling restore across preemptions (R8.2)

`Sched::unlock` proves that after an unlock the ceiling is the maximum over the remaining entries. The remaining entries are exactly those present before the matching lock:

- `lock` pushes at the top and leaves the entries below untouched (proven);
- entries are ordered by holder (invariant 3), so a Job that preempts and later ends removes only its own entries (`complete` and `end_job` leave `lock_res` and `lock_depth` unchanged and shrink `lock_len` only by the top's entries, proven);
- an unlock succeeds only on the top entry owned by the executing Job (proven).

Hence the ceiling after the unlock equals its value before the matching lock.

**Mechanized 2026-10-22 (task 4.13):** `logic::history::lemma_ceiling_restored`. Every step of the ghost history carries a snapshot of the lock stack and the System_Ceiling after it; `history_wf` relates consecutive snapshots by the step's kind (`step_rel`: a lock pushes one entry, an unlock pops the top one, a Job end drops a suffix, the rest keep the stack) and ties each ceiling to its snapshot (`ceiling_of`); `Kernel::inv` ties the last snapshot to the scheduler (`locks_match`), which every entry point maintains and `record` re-establishes from the `Sched` postconditions (`lemma_rel_shrink`, `lemma_rel_lock`, `lemma_rel_unlock`). The theorem: for a lock step `i` and the matching unlock `j` (the first later step at which the stack is back to its depth before `i`, the stack never having gone below the locked entry in between), the stack after `j` is the stack before `i` and the ceiling after `j` equals the ceiling before `i`, whatever other Jobs locked, unlocked, started, or ended in between. The mutation `history-end-forgets-prefix` (a Job end allowed to change entries below the released suffix) fails it. This row of section 3 is now proof evidence at the logic level.

### 5.4 MIT spacing over a run (R10.1, R10.2)

Every accepted Release of a Sporadic_Task sets `last_due` to its own due instant (`accepted`, `deferred_release`, proven). Acceptance requires `now ≥ last_due + MIT` and a deferred Release becomes due at exactly `last_due + MIT` (proven, `Kernel::inv`). Every entry point ensures `mit_spaced`: `last_due` is never forgotten and moves only by at least one MIT (proven per entry point; restarts keep it, R14.5). By induction over the sequence of entry points, consecutive accepted Releases become due at least one MIT apart. `lemma_mit_spaced_trans` is the inductive step for one composition.

**Mechanized 2026-10-22 (task 4.14):** `logic::history::lemma_mit_spacing_over_run`. The trace invariant `history_wf` now states `releases_spaced`: any two Release steps of one Sporadic_Task in the history are due at least one MIT apart. `Kernel::inv` ties the history to the MIT state (`releases_linked`: for a Sporadic_Task, every recorded Release is due no later than `Timing::last_due`, which, once set, is the due instant of a recorded Release); `record` re-establishes both for a Release step from the acceptance rule of `Timing::accept` (`now >= last_due + MIT`) or the deferral clause of `inv` (`deferred_until == last_due + MIT`), through `lemma_release_spaced` and `lemma_release_match_kept`. The theorem reads the invariant back for any two Release steps `i < j` of a Sporadic_Task: `h[j].arg >= h[i].arg + MIT`, so fewer than two Releases of the Task become due in any window shorter than the MIT. The induction the text describes is the one `record` performs at every step. The mutation `mit-run-spacing-dropped` (the invariant weakened to ordering) fails the theorem.

### 5.5 Deadlock freedom (R8.5)

A lock never waits: its outcome depends only on the accessor check, re-entry, and capacity, never on another holder (`lemma_no_other_holder`, proven). No Job suspends inside a Critical_Section (PR-08, PR-09). There is therefore no wait-for edge between Jobs; a pending Job waits only for higher-Priority Jobs to end, which Budget enforcement bounds (P-17).

**Mechanized 2026-10-22 (task 4.14):** `Sched::lemma_deadlock_free`, for every state satisfying `Sched::inv` (so for every state of a run). The waiting relation of the Kernel is `blocked_by(t, i)`: the pending Job of Task `t`, with no Job of `t` in progress and not eligible, is kept from starting by the Job at stack position `i`, which is the executing Job with Priority at least `t`'s or holds a Resource whose Ceiling is at least `t`'s Priority. The theorem states (1) a lock never waits for another Job (`lemma_no_other_holder`, restated), (2) every pending non-eligible Job either waits for its Task's own Job in progress (R9.7) or has a `blocked_by` blocker (the executing Job, or the holder of the entry that attains the System_Ceiling, by `lemma_max_ceiling_attained`), and (3) a blocker is in progress and is blocked by nothing, so every wait-for path has length one and no cycle exists. Not part of the theorem: that waiting ends, which is Budget enforcement (P-17) and the Jobs above ending (`epilogue_core`). The mutation `deadlock-equal-priority-unblocked` (a blocker must have strictly higher Priority) fails clause (2) for a pending Job of the executing Job's Priority. Note the correction to the text above: the blocked Job's Priority may exceed the holder's (bounded inversion); the theorem states the blocker by Ceiling, not by Priority.

### 5.6 Panic freedom under abort (ORQ-07)

- `logic` and `hw_model`: Verus checks every arithmetic operation and index of executable code in range, with no assumption construct. RN-13 notes that Verus has no general mechanism for specifying aborts; the claim is panic freedom, not abort behaviour.
- `arch`, `arch/cm4`, and `rsk-entry`: safe Rust outside `verus!`, with ordinary indexing and arithmetic. It is not proven panic-free. A panic there reaches the single panic handler and the system safe state (R14.4), so it is a detected Kernel fault, not undefined behaviour.
- The Link_Checker's no-panic-path check (task 12.3, 2026-10-23) shows that no Kernel code calls `panic_fmt` or `panic` explicitly (a violation if it did, PR-19) and lists the implicit checks the compiler kept: `panic_bounds_check` reachable through 21 Kernel functions and `panic_const_rem_by_zero` through 2. The Build_System passes the Link_Checker the module paths whose files hold a `verus!` block (`--verified`: `rsk_logic::*`, `rsk_kernel::arch::bound`, `rsk_kernel::arch::cm4::{entry, plan}`), and the checker classifies each caller: 14 of the 21 bounds-check callers and both remainder callers are inside those modules, where the Kernel_Proofs show the index is in range and the divisor nonzero (R44.7), so the compiler's check is unreachable in fact; the other 7 are the shells and stub halves (`Reset`, `rsk_svc_rust`, `rsk_irq_entry_rust`, `rsk_epilogue_rust`, `arch::cm4::fault`) and the demo's safe-state reporter (`print_kv`, `report_and_exit`, Application code that the Kernel's safe state calls), which inline the verified cores; the binary carries no debug information, so a check inside them cannot be attributed to its source. The checks cannot be removed at source level without `unsafe` indexing (PR-20): Verus proves the index in range but the compiler does not use that fact. They remain detected Kernel faults (R14.4), and the stack bound charges the panic path once per entry point.

ORQ-07 stays partly resolved: the explicit-panic half is checked on every Flight_Build; the implicit half is enumerated and classified by proof coverage.

### 5.7 Kernel VFP data moves and R6.6

The DD-01 spike found that LLVM emits `vldr`, `vstr`, and `vmov` for 64-bit data moves in Kernel code on `thumbv7em-none-eabihf`, so the coprocessor stays enabled (hw.rs, ORQ-16 finding). R6.6 as written allows only the context-preservation instructions. These moves do not change a Job's FP context: with eager stacking, entry from a context with an active FP state pushes S0 to S15 and FPSCR before Kernel code runs; S16 to S31 are callee-saved under the AAPCS and are also pushed by the stubs; data moves do not write FPSCR. Resolved 2026-10-22: R6.6 (requirements.md Draft 0.3) and PR-31 (Profile 0.4) were amended to allow floating-point data-movement instructions in Kernel code and to forbid arithmetic, comparison, and conversion instructions and FPSCR writes; GAP-17 is closed. Evidence: the Link_Checker instruction scan (task 12.3, 2026-10-23) classifies every VFP instruction in Kernel code and rejects arithmetic, comparison, conversion, and FPSCR writes; on the P1 demo the only VFP instructions in the Kernel are the `vpush`/`vpop` of the stubs (the compiler emitted no data moves in this build, so the amendment is exercised by the check, not the demo). The HIL register-pattern test is `hil-dt-hw-06` and `-10` (11.8, run 2026-11-02): a Job's registers and FP state across preemptions as HW-06 and HW-10 state.

### 5.8 Stale pending dispatch bits (A4)

When a Release is suppressed after its vector was pended, the NVIC may still take that vector. Taking it clears the pending bit; `irq_entry` finds the Task not eligible and returns unchanged, and the NVIC arbitrates again among the remaining pending vectors, where P-04 applies. Each stale bit is consumed once, so a suppression event causes at most NT stale entries, each costing one stub entry. Whether the Analyzer's Kernel overhead terms include that cost is to be confirmed (task 15.2).

### 5.8a A dispatch vector taken inside another handler's return window (the INVPC of 2026-11-06)

The dispatch stub keeps PRIMASK set from its entry to the instruction before its exception return, and clears it there because a Job runs in Thread mode with PRIMASK clear and an exception return does not restore PRIMASK. Between that clear and the return there is a window of one instruction in which a pending exception of higher priority is taken: at the Kernel level, the time base or an intake, which never dispatch themselves and return into the window; or a dispatch vector of a higher level, which until 2026-11-06 dispatched its Job from there and returned to Thread mode with the preempted dispatch still active. DD-01 as revised forbids exactly that (no Job executes with an exception active; NONBASETHRDENA stays clear), and the processor refuses the return with UFSR.INVPC: the UsageFault of section 13, reproduced three times under the timing configuration and diagnosed on 2026-11-06 by the fault record's processor state (ICSR with RETTOBASE clear, NVIC_IABR0 naming the other Partition's dispatch vector as active, CCR 0x200). The interleaving needs an intake at the Kernel level to have preempted the window first and pended the higher dispatch vector, which the NVIC then takes before the preempted stub's own return; it happened about once an hour under the timing configuration, whose `pulse` edge every 18 ms meets `filter`'s and `control`'s dispatches, and never under the others.

**The first fix was wrong, and the witness build found it (2026-11-07).** The fix of 2026-11-06 made a dispatch vector whose preempted context is a handler (EXC_RETURN bit 3 clear) re-pend itself and return unchanged, expecting the NVIC to take it again after the preempted stub's return. Two hours of the fixed binary ran clean but recorded no entry into the window, so a witness build was made: the rsk-kernel feature `defer-probe` spins in the window (about 150 µs then, 30 µs now; `cargo xtask hil --release-timing --short --defer-probe`), so that the interleaving occurs many times a run instead of once an hour. Its first run showed the re-pend to be a livelock: the re-pended vector has a higher priority than the preempted stub's active exception, so the processor takes it again on the nested return, before the stub's `bx`, and again, 362,283 entries in 0.7 s until the run broke down. A re-pend can never work in the real one-instruction window either; it had simply never run.

**The return window: FAULTMASK closes it.** The stub now executes `cpsid f` before `cpsie i`: FAULTMASK raises the execution priority to -1, so nothing but NMI is taken between the PRIMASK clear and the `bx`, and the exception return itself clears FAULTMASK (ARMv7-M B1.4.3: every exception return other than NMI's), which an exception return does not do for PRIMASK. The pending vector is then taken from Thread mode, after the stub's exception is inactive (or tail-chains from the return, with the frame on the PSP and EXC_RETURN naming Thread mode: the same). The fence is in the dispatch return, the no-change return, and `rsk_unwind`'s return (a dispatch stub that ended a Job unwinds from there at its own level); the Kernel-level handlers need none, since no dispatch vector can preempt them. The cost is one instruction per return. A fault inside the fenced window would escalate to HardFault under FAULTMASK and lock up; the window holds `cpsie i` and the return only, and the return's EXC_RETURN is the plan's constant (the Verus postcondition of `irq_entry_core`), so none is possible there.

**The entry instruction is the other window, and the records' refresh found it (2026-11-07).** With the return fenced, two of the day's four coverage runs (the timing configuration's and the Kernel's) still entered the backstop once each, at a release instant of `control`, whose dispatch vector is the highest. A stub's first instruction is `cpsid i`, and nothing masks a pending exception before it: the exception entry of a dispatch stub A of level L completes with PRIMASK clear, and a vector pended during A's entry (the time base, releasing `control` at that instant, having preempted A there and returned into it) with a priority above L is taken before `cpsid i` executes, with A active and no instruction of A run. That is the interleaving of the finding (the third reproduction's record: `filter`'s vector active with `control`'s dispatching), not the return window, which PRIMASK made a one-instruction target and FAULTMASK closes; the entry window has no instruction to put a mask in front of. **The deferral.** A dispatch vector that finds a handler preempted (EXC_RETURN bit 3 clear) disables itself in the NVIC, records its number in `ArchState::deferred` (eight slots, one per level at most; a ninth is the backstop's Kernel fault, R16.5), traces `window`, counts (`ARCH_WINDOWS`), and returns unchanged into A's entry. A then runs under PRIMASK as every entry does, and the first thing `irq_entry_core` does for an entry taken from Thread mode (bit 3 set: A, never a nested entry, which keeps a vector deferred by a nested entry from being re-taken at A's entry again) is to re-enable and re-pend every deferred vector; PRIMASK holds them to A's return, after which the NVIC takes them from the base level in priority order, where `control`'s dispatch preempts A's Job as the SRP intends. The Kernel's state is untouched by a deferral (the Release stays pending in the NVIC, eligible or not); a Release the Kernel suppresses meanwhile is the stale-bit case of 5.8 when the vector is re-taken. Proven: `irq_entry_core`'s postconditions hold through both loops (Verus); the disable and the re-enable are `Action::Disable` and `Action::Enable` of the Hardware_Model, as the boot's.

**The entry instruction's third path: a Kernel-level entry that ends a Job there (the same day, the witness build's first run with the deferral).** The time base or an intake can preempt a dispatch stub at its entry instruction as well (they return into it, so no deferral is needed), but when such an entry ends the executing Job by a response (a deadline miss or Overrun of `sensor` under the timing configuration's injections) and unwinds, `rsk_unwind` expects the MSP top to be the ended Job's level record (the dispatch stub's pushes, Property 2's LIFO) and finds instead the entry's own exception frame, since the preempted stub has pushed nothing yet; it pops those words as the record and returns to Thread mode with the preempted stub active: UFSR.INVPC again, and the fault record of that run showed it (the preempted stub's vector active, the frame's words garbage). The fixed Kernel owes the unwind to the preempted stub: `irq_entry_core` keeps the level (`ArchState::pending_unwind`, traced as `window` with the level) and returns unchanged; the preempted stub, once it has pushed its own record, pops it, unwinds the owed level, and re-pends its own vector, which the NVIC takes from the resumed context, where it dispatches. The SVC and fault handlers are entered from Thread mode only and never owe one.

**The witness (2026-11-07, `docs/hil/findings/defer_{probe,nofix}_nrf52840_timing_short/`).** The rsk-kernel feature `defer-probe` spins about 30 µs at the stub's entry instruction and 30 µs in its return window (`cargo xtask hil --release-timing --short --defer-probe`), so that both interleavings occur many times a run instead of about once an hour; `defer-nofix` removes the fence and the deferral from the same build. Without them: the first UsageFault 141 ms into the run, 340 fault-record entries (every held one UFSR.INVPC with the diagnostics of the finding), the run broken down in 61 s: the defect reproduced at will. With them: 189 entry-window occurrences in 180 s, each handled (`control`'s dispatch vector deferred and re-taken from Thread mode; `sampler`'s level unwound by the preempted stub on the time base's behalf), no fault, 9,119 pulses answered and none late, 2,184 Kernel actions recorded; the run's one failure is a deadline miss of `control` at 20,540 ms, identical to the cycle in two builds of the witness (the 60 µs the spins add to every dispatch move a `control` Release into `sensor`'s Critical_Section at that phase), a perturbation of the probe and not of the Kernel, which froze the trace as any unexpected event does. The widened windows are charged to the dispatched Job and perturb the timing (the step's bounds are reported, not judged, and no record is written from a witness run); the fence and the deferral check, two instructions and a compare, are inside the Δ_entry the cycle configuration measures, and the records were refreshed on the fixed Kernel. The hour-long and short runs of the campaign stay the evidence that the Flight stub meets no fault; the witness is the evidence that both windows are handled, and the rig's step "dispatch deferrals (5.8a)" reports the deferrals of every timing and stress run, each a handled occurrence of the interleaving. **The campaign of 2026-11-07 (daytime) recorded the first natural occurrences on a Flight stub: four in the hour-long stress run, every one handled, the run passing every rig step with no fault; the hour-long timing run recorded none.**

### 5.10 Endpoint buffers (task 21.2, 2026-11-07)

**What is proven.** `rsk_logic::logic::endpoint::Fifo` is the index state of one directed channel (head, count) of a capacity the table gives; its view is the sequence of occupied slots, oldest first; `push` appends the next free slot and returns it, or returns nothing with the state unchanged when the count is the capacity; `pop` removes the oldest slot and returns it, or nothing when empty; the count never exceeds the capacity (Verus, 333 goals in rsk-logic with it). The Kernel cores `endpoint_send_core` and `endpoint_recv_core` (Verus, inside `svc_core`) copy a message into the slot `push` returns and out of the slot `pop` returns, so the message copied in on the n-th accepted send is the one copied out on the n-th successful receive: nothing lost, duplicated, or reordered (R39.4, Property 11's Endpoint part), as long as each operation is atomic.

**Why each operation is atomic, and R40.6.** Both services run inside the SVC at the Kernel level (DD-09; "with PRIMASK held" until 2026-11-13, which was wrong, audit finding 15: `rsk_svc_entry` has no `cpsid i`, and the atomicity follows from the Kernel level alone, which no Task vector and no other Kernel entry preempts): no Job executes, no other Kernel entry runs, and no Health_Monitor action ends a Job between the index update and the copy. An operation is therefore complete before anything observes it, and R40.6 holds with nothing to undo. The index state lives in `ArchState` without a proven invariant across entries; each core checks `Fifo::is_wf` before the operation and resets a state that fails it, which no Kernel path produces (a restart's re-initialization clears to the empty state, 5.8's arguments untouched).

**What is checked, not proven.** The copy (`hw::copy_words`, through the raw word leaves) and the message glue (`message_to_words`, `message_from_words`, UJ-058 and UJ-059) are under Kani: the copy is total for every argument and copies exactly its words when the ranges are aligned, non-wrapping, and disjoint (as the Endpoint storage and a Partition's RAM are, Property 12), and a message round-trips through the word buffer for every value. The cores admit only buffers inside the calling Partition's RAM interval (`message_permitted`, the mpu model's new predicate) and slots inside the Endpoint storage (`endpoint_slot`): both predicates are proven computations, and the checks are in the cores' code, but that the copy is confined to them is in no contract (corrected 2026-11-13, audit finding 13: `endpoint_args` ensures nothing about the pointer, the length, or `message_permitted`, and `copy_in` and `copy_out` have no `requires`), no mutation, and no negative test with a foreign pointer; it holds by inspection of `endpoint_args` and by the QEMU and HIL runs. The `Plain` bound (UJ-057) is a review item: a marker the primitives and their arrays satisfy by the language's definition, which task 21.4's derive extends.

**What the demo shows.** The QEMU configuration's `telemetry` Endpoint (`control` to `log`, four `u32` each way): 20 sends over the 200 ms, 17 received by the last `log` Job and three in flight at the end, no full channel (`telemetry_full` 0), the Config_Checker's table comparison passing, the App_Prover proving `control`'s body with the send (11 files). The HIL configurations declare no Endpoint; their binaries changed with the Kernel (SVC 8 and 9, the entry kinds `send` and `recv`) and their records are stale until the next campaign (R33.4).

**Since then.** The Protocol and the k-MC bound (5.11), the typestate role APIs and RoleSlots (5.12), the peer-state errors of R42.3 and the `Plain` derive with the receiver-side validation of R42.4 (5.13), and the Analyzer's blocking terms, the WCET_Records of the operations, and the chains (5.14) followed the same day. Still not exercised on a board: a receive-role Release_Source (the path is implemented and proven; the demo's receiver is periodic).

### 5.11 The Protocol_Checker (task 21.1, 2026-11-07)

**What it decides.** `rsk-protocol::check_protocol` accepts a global Protocol for an Endpoint when its structure is well formed (R38.5), every role's projection exists (R38.3, the full merge), and the exploration of every interleaving of the projections over channels of the declared capacity finds no deadlock, no orphan message, no unspecified reception, and no send into a full channel; the largest occupancy of each directed channel in that exploration is its bound k, and the Generator rejects the Endpoint when the Protocol is rejected (R22.7) or the bound exceeds the capacity (R41.1). The exploration is finite: the local types are finite, the channels bounded, and a Protocol beyond 200,000 configurations is rejected as unanalysed rather than trusted.

**Why the bound is universal, not k-MC's (R38.6).** Rumpsteak's k-multiparty compatibility certifies a Protocol by one k under which some k-bounded execution fires every send: a safety argument for unbounded channels. The Kernel's channels hold `capacity` messages and a full one refuses the send and reports the sender (R40.5), so the question for an rsk Application is whether *any* interleaving can fill a channel, which is the universal bound. The two agree on request-response Protocols and differ on streams without a reply, which k-MC accepts at k = 1 and this checker rejects against any capacity; that rejection is the right answer for finite buffers and is what R41.1 asks for. The deviation is recorded in the design (Component F, the reuse table).

**Property 20, the checker's half.** `tests/property20.rs` generates global types over three roles (sequences, choices informing one receiver first, guarded request-response loops) and, for every accepted one: the synchronous trace set of the global type equals that of the product of its projections to depth 6 (the projection is faithful, the reference the typestate API of task 21.3 will be generated from); exploring with capacity k succeeds and with k - 1 fails under R41.1 when k > 1 (the bound is tight); the projections and k do not depend on the order the roles are declared in; and every rejection carries one of the four rules. 300 cases per run, in the workspace tests of every job. The differential run against Rumpsteak's checker that the design named is not performed: Rumpsteak is not vendored (R58.1), and the universal bound would disagree with it by design on exactly the Protocols the Kernel must reject; the differential is against the global type's own semantics instead. Rumpsteak's checker, run out of tree on the same Protocols once a network-connected environment allows it, would still be a useful cross-check of the projection and the merge, and is listed in section 13.

**What the demo shows.** `PingPong` on the QEMU configuration (a request-response loop with a stop branch, between `control` and `log`, capacity 1): accepted, k = 1 on both channels, the projections recorded in the Task_Model (`ctl`: `rec Loop.sink!{Req(u32).sink?{Rep(u32).Loop}, Stop().end}`), the Generated_Config and the Config_Checker's comparison carrying the second Endpoint, the binary running on QEMU as before (the Tasks do not use the Endpoint until task 21.3). The Generator's tests cover the acceptance with the bound, an R41.1 rejection (two requests before a reply against a capacity of 1), an R22.7 rejection naming an unguarded recursion, an unknown Protocol (R21.3), and a Protocol governing no Endpoint (R22.5).

### 5.12 The typestate role APIs (task 21.3, 2026-11-07)

**What the type system gives (R38.4).** A role value in state S has exactly the methods of the projection's actions in S; a send consumes the value and returns the next state or the same state with the reason; a receive returns an enum of the expected labels, each with its payload and next state. Calling an operation the state does not permit is a missing method (E0599), reusing a consumed value a use after move (E0382): the `api-misuse` fixture checks both with `cargo check` on every job (`api_protocol_state`, `api_protocol_reuse`) beside the legal use that compiles (`api_ok`). The State enum and the RoleSlot make the state exact across Jobs (R38.7): a Job that takes a state and stores the state its operations returned cannot store a state the Protocol did not reach, since every state value comes from an operation or from the slot.

**What runs at run time (R41.2).** Rust's types are affine, so a session value can be dropped; the generated `Drop` reports a Protocol violation when the state is neither an end nor a recursion target and no operation consumed the value, the receive reports an unexpected label, and the Job wrapper reports a slot left `Empty`. The report is `SVC_VIOLATION`, recorded as `EV_PROTOCOL_VIOLATION` against the Task; the Partition's state is not changed (the next Job finds the slot `Empty` and the role stays silent until the Partition's restart re-initializes it, which the peer-state rules of task 21.4 will surface to the other role). The QEMU demo's `control` drops its S1 state in its last Job: the safe state counts two violations (the drop, then the empty slot) after the rounds the two roles completed.

**What is not covered.** The role modules were generated code outside the App_Prover's translation at this revision; since 5.13 (the same day) they are translated and proven with the bodies, only their `Drop` report outside it. The HIL protocol-violation cases of the task's evidence line are not injected on the board (no HIL configuration injects a Protocol fault: the QEMU configuration's injected drop is the evidence, and since 5.14 the measurement and response configurations declare the Protocol and play it without injection); listed in section 13.

### 5.13 Cross-Partition rules: the `Plain` derive, receiver-side validation, peer-state errors (task 21.4, 2026-11-07)

**The `Plain` derive (R42.1).** `#[derive(rsk::Plain)]` (rsk-macros) implements the Kernel_Unsafe_Module's `Plain` for a `#[repr(C)]` or `#[repr(transparent)]` struct each of whose fields is itself `Plain`; it refuses enums, unions, and structs without a defined layout with a message naming R42.1, and emits beside the `unsafe impl` a where-clause and one trait-bound assertion per field, so that a field holding a reference, a pointer, a `bool`, or an enum is a compile error of the deriving crate (E0277). The deriving Partition crate keeps `#![forbid(unsafe_code)]` (rustc does not lint the expansion of a derive from another crate); the one `unsafe` keyword outside the Kernel_Unsafe_Module is the derive's `quote!` body, admitted by the PR-20 scan under the new crate policy `unsafe-derive` only when it cites a justification of the Kernel crate's register (UJ-060, a recorded review that argues per field from UJ-057). Evidence: the `api-misuse` fixture's `plain_fields` (E0277 on `&'static u32` and `bool`) and `plain_enum` (the derive's R42.1 errors on an enum and a struct without a layout) members must not compile, `api_ok` derives a `#[repr(C)]` struct of a `u32`, an `[i16; 4]`, and an `f32` and asserts the bound. Trybuild is not vendored (R58.1); the fixture's `cargo check` is the mechanism.

**Receiver-side validation (R42.4).** A role declares its validation function in the Endpoint declaration: `ROLE => PART.TASK validate = PATH` for a plain Endpoint (a `fn(&T) -> bool`), `validate = { LABEL = PATH, .. }` for a Protocol role, one per label it receives with a payload. The Generator requires it for every role that receives from a Partition of lower Criticality_Level (the level letters compare: a channel from C to A is upward; a plain Endpoint has a channel each way, so the demo's `telemetry` declares one on `ctl` although `control` never reads it) and rejects, naming R42.4, a missing function, one for a label the role does not receive with a payload, a labelled function on a plain Endpoint and the reverse. The function is Application code of the receiving Partition, proven total and panic-free by the App_Prover with the Task bodies (level A and B, R46.3); the demo's `validate_rep` carries a postcondition. The Endpoint_Library binds it through the `rsk::Validate<T>` trait (the Generator emits a `rsk_validate::<endpoint>_<role>` implementor as the third parameter of the `Endpoint` Context field; the Protocol role's generated `try_recv` calls it by path): a message the function rejects is consumed, not delivered, `recv` returns `Err(RecvError::Rejected)` (`NotReceived::Rejected` with the state unchanged in the role API), and the library reports it through `SVC_VIOLATION` with the receiving role's identity; the Kernel checks that the executing Task is bound to that role (else API misuse) and records `EV_MESSAGE_REJECTED` (26) against the sending Task, whose Partition receives its declared fault response (`Kernel::fault_of`, Verus: the response applied to the sender's Partition, timing and MIT spacing kept; an END_JOB response with no executing Job of that Partition escalates to the restart, as R18.4's rule for a held Resource). The decision to apply the response, not only to log the event, is R42.4's "fault of the sending Partition" read literally: the integrator chose the sender's response knowing its faults include its invalid messages.

**Peer-state errors (R42.3).** A Partition's stop or restart re-initializes its Endpoint roles (R14.10): both channels of each of its Endpoints are emptied (what it had sent belonged to a state that no longer exists; what it had not read went with its state) and, when the other role belongs to another Partition, that role is marked in the Kernel (`ArchState::peer_changed`, one flag per role, set on the DMA-stop path that both responses take). The marked role's next send or receive returns `ENDPOINT_PEER` once and clears the flag: `SendError::PeerChanged` or `RecvError::PeerChanged` on a plain Endpoint, `NotSent::PeerChanged(role)` or `NotReceived::PeerChanged(role)` in the role API, carrying the role back in its initial state so that both sides start the Protocol over (the restarted side's zeroed slot is that state). The HIL Partition-restart case of the task's evidence line is the response configuration (`part1` restarted at 800 ms by its injected fault): since 5.14 it declares the Endpoints, and the rig's response suite requires `control` to see the peer-state error once on each of its two Endpoints and no rejected reply (the step "peer-state errors after part1's restart"); it runs with the next campaign.

**The QEMU demonstration.** The QEMU configuration's `log` sends `0xFFFF_FFFF` as its fifth `Rep` (the Partition feature `qemu-inject`, enabled by the system crate's `qemu-mps2` with the injected drop of 5.12, never in a HIL build); `control`'s `validate_rep` (`v < 2^31`: a reply echoes a 31-bit count) rejects it at 170 ms; `part1`'s fault response, STOP_PARTITION since this task (RESTART_PARTITION before: a restart would zero the counters the safe state reports, a stop keeps them, and the demo's `part1` faults nowhere else), stops it; `control`'s next telemetry send and PingPong receive each report the peer-state change once; the safe state requires exactly one `EV_MESSAGE_REJECTED` against `log`, one `EV_PARTITION_STOP` of `part1`, `pingpong_rejected`, `pingpong_peer`, and `telemetry_peer` each 1, the four completed rounds, and the two Protocol violations of 5.12 (`RSK-OK`; `cargo xtask qemu`).

**The role APIs under the App_Prover.** The generated role modules compile under `cfg(creusot)` since this task: their Kernel glue is `rsk`'s trusted functions (`words::{zero, message_to_words, message_from_words}`, the Endpoint's send and receive), their loops carry Creusot invariants and variants, the initial state is a `const fn` (a `const` item would be made transparent and refuse the private constructor), and only the `Drop` report is outside the translation (`#[cfg(not(creusot))]`); the bodies' Protocol steps are no longer gated. `p1-demo-part0-proof` proves 25 files (11 before: `validate_rep`, the role modules' send, receive, and state code, and the bodies' Protocol steps), `part0-measure` 25 and `part0-response` 28 with their configurations' Endpoints.

**Proof and check status.** Verus: rsk-logic 335 (`fault_of`), rsk-kernel 177 (`violation_core`, the peer flags in the send and receive cores, the re-initialization on the stop path); Kani harnesses unchanged (the copy and the message glue); Creusot as above; `cargo xtask check` 0 violations (PR-20's new `unsafe-derive` policy and the R6.4 register with UJ-060); the QEMU demo passes.

### 5.14 Endpoint operations in the Analyzer and end-to-end chains (task 21.5, 2026-11-07)

**The operations as Critical_Sections (R40.2).** Two Kernel_Timing_Parameters, Δ_send and Δ_recv (`delta_send`, `delta_recv`: the schema, `rsk-model`, the rig's entry-parameter table from entry kinds 16 and 17, and the Analyzer's placeholders), are the WCET_Records of the operations (R39.3). For each Endpoint role of a Task the Analyzer adds two Critical_Section sites at the Kernel level (a send and a receive of Δ_send and Δ_recv, the Ceiling above every Priority, DD-09) to the blocking terms of every Task above (`terms::Site::endpoint`: such a site blocks for its WCET in every case, since the operation runs to its end inside the SVC, R40.6, where a Resource site of a stopping Partition blocks for Budget + Δ_detect + Δ_enforce; it costs no lock entry and exit; it counts for L_i), and charges in the Task's O_i its declared operations per Job (`ops = N` on the role, 1 unless declared; the demo's `log` drains up to the capacity, `ops = 4`) times the larger of the two (`TaskIn::endpoint_cost`); `terms.rs` is re-verified (Verus, 68). The report lists the approximation: the count per Job is the declaration's, the parameters the measured maxima over the configuration's payload sizes. With the placeholder timing the demo's B_i are unchanged (L_kernel's floor dominates) and `control`'s O_i grows by its two roles' cost; the golden report records it.

**End-to-end chains (R41.4).** `chain NAME { PART.TASK -> PART.TASK [-> ..] } deadline = T;` declares an interaction chain; the Generator requires each consecutive pair to be the two roles of one Endpoint, a Sporadic_Task after the first to be released by that Endpoint's role under the `defer` policy (another Release_Source, or `discard`, leaves when the message is read unbounded by the chain), records the chain in the Task_Model (`chains`, hops and the deadline in ticks) and the summary. The Analyzer's bound is the sum of: Δ_stamp when the chain starts at an interrupt event; the first Task's WCRT; for each later Task its period (a Periodic_Task samples the Endpoint once per period) or its MIT (a Sporadic_Task released by the arrival under `defer`: an arrival inside the window is released at its end, R10.2) and its WCRT; PASS only when every Task of the chain is schedulable and the bound is within the deadline, UNBOUNDED (a FAIL) for a hop the rules above do not bound, and a rejection naming R41.4 for a hand-edited chain the model's Endpoints do not connect (`terms::checked_sum`, Verus; `lib.rs`). The report carries the chains in JSON (`chains`, schema extended) and text ("End-to-end chains (R41.4)"), each term named. The demo's `telemetry_path` (`control` to `log`, 100 ms) bounds at R_control + T_log + R_log = 2,148,050 cycles (85.9 ms) with the placeholder timing.

**Evidence.** Unit tests (`rules.rs`): `r40_2_endpoint_operations_are_kernel_level_critical_sections` (the site's WCET against the Budget rule, the lock-cost exclusion, O_i), `r40_2_the_demo_charges_the_declared_operations_per_job` (5 and 2 operations; a Δ_send above the floor becomes the named blocking term), `r41_4_chain_bounds_follow_the_hops` (the demo's bound; a sporadic receiver under `defer` and `discard`; another Release_Source; an interrupt-started chain's Δ_stamp; the rejection). PBT against the simulator (`properties.rs`, `r41_4_simulated_chain_latency_never_exceeds_the_bound`, 300 sets): the discrete SRP simulator now traces every Job's release, first execution, and completion; for every ordered pair of Tasks of a set the Analyzer finds schedulable, the latency from a sender's Release to the completion of the first receiver Job that starts after the sender's completion never exceeds R_sender + T_receiver + R_receiver, and comes within a period of it for some pairs (not vacuous). The measured values of Δ_send and Δ_recv come from the plain measurement configuration, which declares the Endpoints since this task (the Partition crates' `telemetry` feature through the system crate's `endpoints`); the other records carry them as placeholders (the measured analyses overlay the measured parameters on placeholders, task 19.3).

**Measured (the campaign of 2026-11-07/08, `nrf52840_measure`, 180 s; the record of the re-tuned binary).** Δ_send 2,166 cycles observed (33.8 µs; 27,001 sends; 2,708 with the margin; 2,174 in the campaign's first run), Δ_recv 751 cycles (11.7 µs; 35,997 receives; 939 with the margin), each the Rust half of its SVC over the configuration's payloads of 4 and 8 bytes; L_kernel 16,427 cycles observed (the span now covering them). **Finding, closed the same day.** With the operations charged (R40.2: `control`'s two roles × Δ_send per Job, `log`'s six operations, the sink of `pingpong` performing a receive and a reply per Job, `ops = 2`) the measured analysis of the plain configuration, a PASS before, put `part1.log` 58 µs and `part2.sampler` 1.3 ms over their deadlines at 98 % utilization (observed response maxima 4.0 and 4.3 ms: the pessimism is the Budgets as C_i, the lowest Tasks paying every higher Budget). The measurement configuration's `sensor` Budget was re-tuned from 8.8 to 8.25 ms (its measured Job is about 6.4 ms, 7.9 ms with the margin, so R28.4 holds with 0.35 ms headroom; the QEMU and the other HIL configurations keep theirs), after which the same records give PASS with `log` at 1.0 ms of slack and `sampler` at 60 ms (the recurrence no longer near its divergence), the chain `telemetry_path` bounding at 88.3 ms within 100 ms. The run repeated on the re-tuned binary after the campaign (2026-11-08): PASS at 95 % utilization, `log` 38.9 ms within 40, `sampler` 39.7 ms within 100, every observed response within its WCRT, the record refreshed (`analysis-rta-nrf52840_measure-measured`).

**The campaign's other findings (2026-11-08).** The hour-long timing run (`nrf52840_timing`, 823,317 periodic Releases, 203,333 pulses answered with none late) failed its two judgements by a hair, neither a change of this task's code (the configuration declares no Endpoint): (1) one `sampler` Job charged 32,617 cycles against its Budget of 500 µs (32,000; `EV_OVERRUN`, the trace frozen at it: the Job preempted by `log`'s 3 ms Job and interrupted throughout, each entry's Δ_entry charged to it), where the previous hour-long record's measured maximum of 31,477 cycles (39,347 with the margin) already exceeded that Budget under R28.4; the Budget is 650 µs in the timing, response, isolation, and nRF52840 base configurations since (the measurement configuration had 600 µs, the stress ones 800 µs); (2) a release lag of 10,072 cycles (157 µs) once, against the rig's expectation of 10,000 (the previous maximum 7,673): a release's lag is bounded by the masked span preceding its time-base entry (21,812 cycles observed in the same run), so the expectation, a regression guard rather than a claim, is 12,500 since (`hil-rig.toml`); the Analyzer uses the measured maximum with the margin (12,590 cycles). The hour-long records are stale until the next campaign (R33.4: not used); the short ones were refreshed on the final binary.

### 5.15 Communication deadlock freedom (R41.3; task 21.6, 2026-11-08, revised the same day)

**What "deadlock" means here.** No Job ever blocks: a send into a full channel is refused (R40.5), a receive from an empty one returns empty (R40.4), and every Job runs to completion (PR-08, PR-09). The only state that persists is each role's Protocol state in its RoleSlot between Jobs (R38.7) and each channel's content. A role *awaits* a message when its state offers operations, each a receive, none enabled (its channel is empty, or its oldest message carries another label): the Task polls and finds nothing, within its Protocol or for the arrival that is its next Release (R40.3). A role has *finished* when its state offers no operation (an `end`): it will never send again. Communication deadlock is therefore a liveness property over role states across Jobs: a set of roles that will never communicate again because each awaits a message that only a role of the set can send, where a finished role counts as one that can send nothing.

**The per-Endpoint theorem (`rsk-protocol::progress`, Verus, 44 items).** For one Protocol-governed Endpoint, with the roles' projections as automata (`Trans`), FIFO channels per ordered pair, `enabled`, `succ`, `step`, and runs from `initial` (every role in state 0, every channel empty):

- `deadlock_set(s, c, set)`: a set of roles with at least one awaiting member, every member awaiting or finished, and the sender of every awaited channel in the set; `deadlocked(s, c)` its existence (strengthened on 2026-11-08 to count finished senders: a wait on a peer that has ended is a deadlock too).
- `pub proof fn theorem_r41_3(s: System, list: Seq<Cfg>) requires certificate(s, list) ensures forall|c: Cfg| reachable(s, c) ==> !deadlocked(s, c) && within_capacity(s, c)`: in plain words, every run of the Endpoint reaches only configurations with no waiting set and no channel over the declared capacity, provided the explorer's visited set is a certificate (it lists the initial configuration, every listed configuration is well formed, within the capacity, and not deadlocked, and every enabled operation of a listed configuration leads to a listed one).
- `pub fn certify(s: &System, list: &Vec<Config>) -> (r: bool) ensures r ==> certificate(..) && (forall reachable c: !deadlocked && within_capacity)`: the verified check of the explorer's set, which `check_protocol` requires of every accepted Protocol (a refusal names R41.3). `has_deadlock` decides `deadlocked` by the greatest fixed point of the awaiting and finished roles under "every awaited sender is in the set", with the member count as its measure.

**The system theorem (`rsk-protocol::compose`, Verus, 3 items; the A-P8 assumption made a theorem).** A Task's Jobs may make an operation on one Endpoint wait for a receive on another; the declaration states this as a gating relation between Protocol-governed Endpoints (`ROLE => PART.TASK after = ENDPOINT`: the Task's operations on this role may wait for its receives on `ENDPOINT`, another Protocol Endpoint of the same Task), and the Generator accepts a declaration only when the relation is acyclic over the Application, which it certifies by ranks (every edge rises in rank; `gating_rank` in the Task_Model). A *stuck set* (`stuck_set`) is a set of Tasks with a Task awaiting on some Endpoint, in which every Task's enabled operation on an Endpoint is gated by one of its own awaiting roles on an Endpoint that gates it, and the sender of every awaited message is a Task of the set or a finished role.

- `pub proof fn theorem_system_progress(ms: Seq<Member>, cs: Seq<Cfg>, gates: spec_fn(int, int) -> bool, ntasks: int) requires system_wf(ms, cs, ntasks), ranked(ms, gates), forall|e| !deadlocked(ms[e].sys, cs[e]) ensures forall|set: Seq<bool>| !stuck_set(ms, cs, gates, ntasks, set)`: when no Endpoint's configuration is deadlocked on its own and the gating is ranked, no set of Tasks is stuck. Proof: take the lowest-ranked Endpoint on which a Task of the set awaits; the set's awaiting roles and the finished roles on it form a waiting set of that Endpoint, since an awaited sender with an enabled operation would be a Task of the set able on this Endpoint, hence gated by an awaiting role on a lower-ranked one, against the choice.
- `pub proof fn theorem_system_r41_3(ms, lists, gates, ntasks) requires forall|e| member_wf(ms[e]) && certificate(ms[e].sys, lists[e]), ranked(ms, gates) ensures forall|cs| (forall|e| reachable(ms[e].sys, cs[e])) ==> forall|set| !stuck_set(..)`: with a certificate for every Endpoint, no combination of reachable Endpoint configurations has a stuck set. The gated system's reachable configurations are among those combinations, since gating only removes interleavings and each Endpoint's steps are a run of its own.

**The Kernel link (goal 3; `rsk-kernel::arch::cm4::entry`, Verus, 180 items).** The send and receive cores perform their channel step through `channel_push` and `channel_pop`, whose postconditions are the model's `succ`: a send appends the next free slot to the channel's view (`Fifo::view`, Property 11) or changes nothing when the channel holds `capacity` messages (`slot.is_none() ==> count == cap`, which is A-P3 as a lemma: a send is never refused below the capacity, and the certificate shows no run reaches it); a receive removes the oldest slot (`drop_first`) or changes nothing when empty; no other channel and no peer-state flag changes. A rejected message now re-initializes its Endpoint in the Kernel (`endpoint_reinit_one`: both channels emptied, both roles marked, so that each role's next operation returns it to the initial state through the peer-state error, R42.3): the Endpoint is then in the initial configuration, which every certificate lists, whatever the sending Partition's fault response. The message in a slot is the words the Kernel copied into it (`Action::Copy`, Kani, UJ-056 to UJ-059), and its first word is the label (5.12): that correspondence between the slot sequence and the label sequence is the glue the link rests on.

**The assumptions, restated with their status.**

- A-P1, the roles follow their projections, and each Job performs the enabled operations of its ungated roles (finding 4 of the review). Since the evening of 2026-11-09 the role modules are rendered from the verified state tables and transitions (`Checked::tables`, `Checked::trans`; `roles::states_from_tables`), whose agreement with the projection is `theorem_two_role_automaton` and `theorem_multiparty_automaton`, so the states and operations of the typestate API are the projection's by theorem; the typestate API makes any other operation a type error (R38.4), the role modules' bodies are proven by the App_Prover (5.13), the state persists in the RoleSlot (R38.7); a dropped or unstored role value or an unexpected label is reported at run time (R41.2), after which the claim holds again once the Endpoint is re-initialized (A-P6). Remains for the rendering of the tables into source text and for finding 4, which is about the Application's bodies, outside the Verus model.
- A-P2, the channels are the model's: a lemma for the slot sequence (`channel_push`, `channel_pop`, `Fifo::view`; Property 11), and since the evening of 2026-11-09 a lemma for the label sequence too (`send_labels_ok`, `recv_labels_ok`: the model's `store` of first words, the architecture's `copy_in`/`copy_out`). The step the Kernel cannot see, that the role API puts the label in the first word, is an App_Prover obligation since the night of 2026-11-09 (`rsk::Endpoint::send_words`' contract establishes `rsk::channel::sent(id, role, peer, w[0])`, and every generated `send_<label>` ensures `sent(ID, ROLE, PEER, LABEL)`; the receive side likewise, `recv_words` and `try_recv` with `channel::received`), cited from `copy_in`'s assumption. What remains is the hardware assumption: nothing but `copy_in` writes the storage (R16.1, the MPU).
- A-P3, a send never waits: a lemma (`channel_push`'s refusal clause with `within_capacity`).
- A-P4, waiting is the model's waiting: by construction of the Profile (PR-08, PR-09: no Job suspends), with the `discard` MIT policy noted as in 5.14, and every Job running (schedulability, P-18; finding 4). A Task released by a message arrival awaits it before every operation: a Generator rule since the review (finding 3). Remains a property of the Profile rather than a Verus lemma.
- A-P5, Resources: a lemma already (`Sched::lemma_deadlock_free`, Property 6).
- A-P6, re-initialization returns both roles to the initial configuration: a postcondition for the channels, the flags, and since 2026-11-13 the delivery latches of `endpoint_reinit_one` (a rejected message, a Protocol violation); for a Partition's stop or restart (`endpoint_reinit`, `clear_channels`) no postcondition exists and the claim is a standing assumption with test evidence (corrected 2026-11-13, audit finding 14: those two are verified for bounds only; the HIL response configuration's peer-state step exercises them, and the coverage of `endpoint_reinit` on the Target was 0 of 16 branches in the measurement configurations); the role API's return to `S0` on the peer-state error is generated code under the App_Prover (5.13).
- A-P7, rejected messages: closed by the Kernel rule above (goal 2); the Generator rule of the morning (a validated role's peer Partition had to declare STOP_PARTITION or RESTART_PARTITION) is withdrawn, since the semantics now cover every fault response. The cost is one more branch in the `SVC_VIOLATION` entry, two channel clears and two flag writes, on a path no measurement scenario exercises (no board configuration injects a rejection). The short runs of 2026-11-08 on the changed Kernel (timing-short, stress-short, and the plain measurement configuration, 180 s each, all passed by the rig; the stress configuration's measured analysis FAIL at 97 % is its documented claim of 11.14, the protected Partition alone) read L_kernel at 18,782 cycles (timing-short; 19,694 before), 24,878 (stress-short; unchanged), and 17,600 (measure; 20,534 before), Δ_send 2,708 with the margin (unchanged) and Δ_recv 914 (939 before): the channel helpers compile to the code the cores had inline, and the differences are run-to-run spread. Schedulability is unchanged: the measurement configuration's analysis PASSes at 95 % with every observed response within its WCRT; the hour-long records were not refreshed (R33.4: stale).
- A-P7, the consequence (a stated limitation, 2026-11-08): because a rejected message re-initializes the Endpoint, a sender that keeps sending invalid messages keeps resetting the conversation, and the Protocol may never advance past its initial state. R41.3 holds (no role waits for a message only a waiting role can send: both roles are returned to the initial configuration, where the sender can send), progress under repeated faults does not. The resets are bounded: each costs the sender one message, so their rate is at most the sender's own operation rate (its Jobs' Release rate, bounded by its period or MIT, times its `ops`), and the receiving role's Job rate (its period or MIT) bounds how often the receiver sees one; the sending Partition's fault response applies at each rejection (R42.4): STOP_PARTITION ends the sequence at the first rejection, SAFE_STATE ends the Application, and RESTART_PARTITION bounds it by one restart per rejection (the Partition's restart work of R14.10 is in the Analyzer's blocking terms); END_JOB is escalated by the Kernel to the restart, since no Job of the sending Task is executing when the receiver rejects (`Kernel::fault_of`), so every fault response of R14.5 bounds the resets by the restart cost or ends them. The receiving Task's deadlines are unaffected: a rejection is one receive operation of its Job, charged as Δ_recv (R40.2), whatever the message's validity. **Evidence (2026-11-08):** the QEMU variant `qemu_flood` (`apps/p1-demo/decl/src/qemu_flood.rs`: the QEMU declaration with `part1`'s `fault = RESTART_PARTITION` and 500 ms; `cargo xtask qemu --flood`), in which `log` answers every request with an invalid reply (feature `qemu-flood`): 6 rejections in 500 ms, one every 80 ms (`log` answers every 40 ms, and the Job after a re-initialization sees the peer-state error instead of a request), each matched by a peer-state error on `control` (6) and by one restart of `part1` (6, at 11, 91, 171, 251, 331, and 411 ms); `control` ran its 50 Jobs with no deadline miss and no miss anywhere; `pingpong_reps` stayed 0, the Protocol never advancing past a round; one `EV_ENDPOINT_FULL` of the plain `telemetry` Endpoint as a restart shifted `log`'s drain against `control`'s sends (R40.5, the word not taken). The safe state requires all of it (rejections at least 3, every rejection matched, the restarts equal to the rejections, no miss of `control`, no round completed).
- A-P8, one Endpoint at a time: closed as a theorem (`theorem_system_r41_3`) for the declared gating, and its body half an App_Prover obligation since the evening of 2026-11-08. **The obligation.** For every Endpoint `E` that gates a role of a Task (some role of the Task declares `after = E`), the generated Context carries a gate `<E>_gate: rsk::Gate`, constructed closed by the Job wrapper at every Job's start; the body's generated precondition (`rsk_spec::<task>_pre`) states every gate closed; `E`'s role module implements `rsk::Received` for its receive outcomes (`RecvS*`), and `Gate::open(&outcome)` is the only way to open a gate (the field is private, the model opaque, the three operations trusted contracts: closed at construction, open after `open`, read by `is_open`); every operation of a role declared `after = E` (`send_*`, `try_recv`) takes `gate_E: &rsk::Gate` with the precondition `requires((*gate_E)@)`. So a body can operate on a gated role only after a receive on `E` completed in the same Job and was presented to the gate; a body that operates ungated fails its proof at that call. What the obligation does not say: that the body performs the gated operation when the gate is open (liveness of the body, A-P1's kind), nor anything for Endpoints without `after` (independent roles: no gating is claimed, and none is needed by the theorem). **Evidence.** The demo's `log` (`relay` after `pingpong`) and `filter` (`report` after `relay`) open their gates from the receive outcome and pass them to the gated operations (the App_Prover members part0, part2, part0-measure, and part2-measure prove the bodies with the obligation in force, 36, 37, 36, and 38 files on 2026-11-08; `log` had no proof member until the QEMU `part1` member of 2026-11-09 and the eight HIL members of Partition 1 the same night, 7; its gated body is proven since under the obligation, with `rsk_spec::log_pre`). The fixture `xtask/fixtures/gating-misuse` declares two Protocol Endpoints on the same Tasks with `pong`'s role gated by `ping`, and a body that sends on `pong` without a receive on `ping`: it compiles and runs, and the App_Prover refuses it at that call (`proof failed`, goal `vc_alpha`, 29 of 30 goals proven, 2026-11-08; `proofs/creusot-negative`, a workspace of proofs that must fail, run by the job's App_Prover step, which errors if such a proof ever succeeds). Plain Endpoints carry no Protocol state and no progress claim; a Protocol role gated by a plain Endpoint's receive is outside the theorem and the declaration refuses it (fixture `api_gating_plain`; a cycle and a self-gating role are refused likewise, `api_gating_cycle`, `api_gating_self`).
- A-P9, the checker's inputs: for every role count the automaton is the verified flattening of the projection of the parsed syntax (`theory::flatten_two`, `multiparty::flatten_n`, 2026-11-09; findings 1 and 2 closed), and the parser (`parse.rs`) is the one unverified step before it; the role modules are generated from `roles::states` over that projection (R38.3, finding 12), and the conversion `to_theory` relies on `well_formed` having rejected a choice branch to another receiver (finding 10); the Property 20 traces, the certified count, the Generator's tests, and the `api-misuse` fixture are its checks. Remains as glue for the parser and the role modules.
- A-P10, tools: Verus and Z3 (TBR-12), vstd (TBR-04); the explorer is not trusted.

**The independent review of the statements (2026-11-08, night).** A reviewer given only R38 to R41 and the theorem statements (no proofs, no code) reported fifteen findings; their dispositions:
- Finding 3 (the Release half of R41.3 unmodelled: two sporadic Tasks each released by the other's message, each Protocol beginning with that Task's send, pass every theorem yet neither is ever released): **accepted and closed by a Generator rule the same night** (`validate.rs`, test `r41_3_a_message_release_gates_the_tasks_other_endpoints`): a Task released by an arrival on a Protocol Endpoint awaits that arrival before every operation, so its role there must begin with a receive (a first send is refused under R41.3: the Task would await its own message), and the releasing Endpoint gates the Task's other Protocol Endpoints (an implicit `after`, in the ranks, the acyclicity check, and the generated gates, so the obligation of A-P8 applies to it); with it the counterexample is a cycle `e1 -> e2 -> e1` and refused. A release by a plain Endpoint's arrival is outside the theorem (plain Endpoints carry no claim; A-P4).
- Findings 1, 2, 12 (nothing stated connects the certified automaton, the tables `flattens` speaks of, and the generated typestate API to the projection): **accepted; 1 and 2 closed for two roles on 2026-11-09 by the verified flattening and exploration** (above), 12 named in A-P9: the flattening of `explore::certify` (plain Rust) is the one unverified step between the projection and the automaton the certificate and the bridge take; the role modules are generated from `roles::states(local)` over the same projection (R38.3), with the Generator's tests and the `api-misuse` fixture as evidence; both are section 13 items.
